--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The champion (score 10057.60) uses a 3-state FSM:
- State 0: Attraction (moving toward robots) - with transitions on perceiving colors 5/6 and few neighbors
- State 1: Exploration (random walk, low turning rate 4) - with transitions on white floor, and random probability
- State 2: Stop - with transitions on perceiving color 3 and many neighbors

The second best is a single-state Stop (10120), which is very close to the champion. This makes sense: before the switch, being stationary is good; after the switch, moving is good. The challenge is detecting the switch.

The green wall lights up at switch time. Color 2 = green (likely). The champion doesn't seem to explicitly use green detection. The key insight: robots need to Stop before the switch and Move after the switch. The green wall (color 2) is the signal.

**Strategy:**
- Before switch: Stop → minimize score
- After switch: Move (Exploration/Attraction) → minimize score
- Signal: Green wall appears (color 2) at switch time

**Design philosophy:**
1. Start in Stop state
2. When green is perceived (C=7, l=2), transition to movement state
3. Possibly go back to stop if green disappears? No - once green appears, stay moving.

The best simple design: 2 states - Stop and Exploration, with transition from Stop to Exploration on perceiving green (color 2).

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Champion (exact copy)** - must include
2. **Simple 2-state: Stop → Explore on green** - intuitive solution
3. **Simple 2-state: Stop → Exploration** with green detection, high probability
4. **3-state with explicit green detection**
5-8. **Variants of champion with parameter tweaks**
9-14. **Exploration of different topologies**

For color mapping: green = color 2 (from the problem, GoToColor uses clr in {1..6}). The arena wall lights up GREEN. Let me use l=2 for green in C=7 conditions.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Rounds 1-2:**

1. **Champion** (9723.33): 3-state FSM with Attraction→Exploration→Stop pattern. The critical insight is state 1 (Exploration, rwm=4) has a transition triggered by perceiving color 2 (green) with p=0.8, which causes it to stay in exploration mode after the green wall appears.

2. **Single-state Stop** (9785.85) is very competitive - nearly as good as the champion. This suggests the scoring is dominated by the "before switch" phase where stopping is optimal.

3. **Green detection machines** (ranks 5-6, scores 11421-11604) perform noticeably worse than pure stop or the champion. The green-to-movement transition approach isn't working as well as expected.

4. The large gap between rank 4 (9841) and rank 5 (11421) is significant - only the top 4 machines are competitive.

**Why does the champion work?**
- State 0 (Attraction): robots cluster, reducing movement accidentally
- State 1 (Exploration with low rwm=4): very slow turning, almost straight movement
- State 2 (Stop): pure stillness
- The transitions seem to create a system where robots tend toward stopping before the switch

**Strategy for Round 3:**
- Keep champion (elitism)
- Explore variants: what if state 2 (Stop) has a green-triggered transition to exploration?
- Try adding green detection to the stop state in the champion topology
- Try pure Stop variants
- Try 2-state Stop+Exploration where exploration is triggered by green with high probability and very low false-positive rate
- The key problem with green-detection machines: they might transition too early or the green detection doesn't reliably work

Let me think more carefully. The champion's state 1 (Exploration) has transition `--n1x3 1 --c1x3 7 --p1x3 0.8 --l1x3 2` - this transitions to state 1 itself... wait, destination 1 from state 1 means it goes to state 2 (since state 1's others are [0,2], destination 1 = state 2). Actually with 3 states, leaving state 1, others are [0, 2], so D=0→state 0, D=1→state 2. So `--n1x3 1` means transition to state 2 (Stop) when perceiving color 2!

So the champion actually transitions TO STOP when green is perceived! That seems counterintuitive... unless the green wall appears and robots stop (which is wrong after switch). Let me re-examine.

Wait - perhaps the green detection is acting as a noise-rejection or the transitions fire probabilistically and the net effect is complex. The champion has many transitions from state 1, creating a complex dynamic.

Let me focus on what's working: the champion topology is clearly best. I should:
1. Copy champion exactly
2. Make small parameter variations of the champion
3. Try adding green→move transition to stop state
4. Try the stop-only machine
5. Some genuine alternatives

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

The top 7 machines all score between 9723-10171, and they are all variants of the champion topology (3-state: Attraction→Exploration→Stop). There's a meaningful gap after rank 7 (10171→10225 for pure Stop).

**What's working:**
- The champion topology: State 0=Attraction(att=4.0647), State 1=Exploration(rwm=4), State 2=Stop
- State 1 transitions: white floor (p=0.9826), two random (p~0.15-0.25), color 2 perception (p=0.8)
- State 2 transitions: color 3 perception (p=0.7095), many neighbors (threshold 6-7, w=13-15)
- Key improvements: lower p1x1/p1x2 (0.15 vs 0.25) and higher p2x1 threshold (7 vs 6) seem to help

**Parameter trends from top machines:**
- Best (9996): p1x1=0.15, p1x2=0.15, p2x1=7
- 2nd (10059): added color2 detection from state 0 (p=0.6)
- 3rd (10103): original champion params
- 4th (10111): p0x1=5 (neighbor threshold)
- 6th (10146): p0x1=3 (neighbor threshold)

**Strategy for Round 4:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Exploit rank-1 of this round (lower probabilities 0.15/0.15, threshold 7)
3. Combine best features: lower p1x1/p1x2 + add color2 from state 0 + threshold=7
4. Try even lower random transition probabilities (0.10, 0.12)
5. Try different neighbor thresholds in state 2
6. Try adding green detection to state 2 (transition to exploration when green seen)
7. Some topological exploration

The key insight: lower random transition probabilities from state 1 (exploration) means robots stay in exploration longer → less time wasted in attraction state → better score. The color 2 perception in state 0 helps too.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 5:**

The top machine (9781.88) uses p1x1=p1x2=0.05, with the 4-transition state 0 including color 2 detection (p=0.6), and threshold 7 in state 2. This is better than p=0.07 (rank 2) and p=0.10 (ranks 3-13).

The trend is clear: **lower random transition probabilities from state 1 are better**. Let me try even lower: p=0.02, p=0.03, p=0.01.

**Convergence analysis:**
- The topology is essentially fixed: 3-state, Attraction→Exploration→Stop
- State 0: 4 transitions including color 2 (p=0.6), color 5 (p=0.75), color 6 (p=0.28), few-neighbors
- State 1: rwm=1-4, white floor (p=0.9826), two random (p~0.05), color 2 (p=0.8)
- State 2: color 3 (p=0.71), many-neighbors (threshold 7-8, w=13.817)

**What to explore:**
1. Push p1x1/p1x2 to 0.01, 0.02, 0.03 (lower is better trend)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Major breakthrough in Round 6!**

The champion jumped from 9723 to 7830! The key difference in the new champion:
- State 1 has only **3 transitions** (not 4): white floor (p=0.9826), random (p=0.03), **color 2 perception (p=0.8)**
- The two random transitions were collapsed into one, and the color 2 transition replaced the second random one
- This is fundamentally different: instead of two weak random exits, there's one random exit + one green-triggered exit

**Why this works:**
- Before switch: robots in state 1 (Exploration) rarely exit randomly (p=0.03), so they explore with white floor trigger → state 2 (Stop) via p=0.9826 on white floor
- After switch: green wall appears → color 2 perception fires (p=0.8) → transition back to state 1 (destination index 1 from state 1 with 3 states = state 2... wait)

Let me re-examine: State 1 has 3 transitions: n1x0→1 (state 2=Stop on white floor), n1x1→0 (state 0=Attraction, random p=0.03), n1x2→1 (state 2=Stop on color 2 p=0.8). So green perception sends robots to Stop! Before switch, robots stop on white floor and also when they see green (which doesn't appear yet). After switch, green appears and robots stop even faster.

This makes sense! The green wall signal makes robots stop MORE reliably after the switch - but wait, the score penalizes moving after the switch. So stopping is ALWAYS good? 

Actually re-reading: score increases by 1 for every MOVING robot after switch. So stopping after switch is GOOD. The green wall signal helps robots stop faster/reliably after the switch. Before switch, stopping is also good. So this FSM makes robots stop very efficiently.

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Try p1x1 (random) even lower: 0.01, 0.02
- Try p1x2 (color 2 to stop) at different values: 0.9, 0.95, 0.7
- Try different color detection in state 1
- Try removing the random transition entirely (2 transitions from state 1)
- Try p0x3 (color 2 from state 0) at different values
- Try different w values for state 2 neighbor threshold

## Reasoning

**Excellent progress! Champion at 5861.51 with p1x1=0.005**

Key observations from Round 8:
- p=0.005 wins (5861) over p=0.01 (6500) and p=0.001 (6687)
- So 0.005 is a sweet spot - not too low (0.001 is worse) and not too high (0.01 is worse)
- rwm=2 with p=0.01 gives 6214 - interesting, lower rwm helps with p=0.01
- p0x1=5 (neighbor threshold 5 instead of 4) with p=0.01 gives 6440 - slightly better than p=0.01 baseline
- p1x2=0.95 with p=0.01 gives 6462 - slightly worse than 0.8

**What to explore:**
1. Combine p=0.005 with rwm=2, rwm=1 (lower turning = more stopping tendency)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
